That's where the NFT craze originated. CryptoKitties, a combination of Tamagotchi and trading cards, burst onto the scene in 2017. Each kitten is unique and can be raised, replicated, and sold for up to $140,000.

NFT mania was born, and interest in NFTs is increasing. Why Are 

Non-Fungible Tokens (NFTs) Essential to Brands?

The ability to represent digital files such as art, audio, and video is one of the key reasons NFTs are valuable to brands. They're so adaptable that they can be used to represent other forms of creative work like virtual worlds, virtual real estate, fashion, and much more.

What does this have to with your brand and marketing strategy?

NFTs have opened up new forms of brand storytelling and consumer interaction, which are the two key pillars of a successful marketing campaign, thanks to the global interest they've created.

With NFTs, you can:

•  Increase brand awareness

•  Create unique brand experiences

•  Encourage interaction

•  Create interest in your product and brand

 

Finally, NFTs will assist you in increasing conversions and generating revenue.

Here are some examples of how companies are using NFTs in their marketing activities.

Six Ways Brands Are Using NFTs

The concept of NFTs in marketing can be difficult to understand. The easiest way to explain the most complex concepts is to look at examples.

Here are a few creative ways brands are utilizing NFTs. Hopefully, you will get some inspiration from them.

1.  Taco Bell GIFs

![](Images/image00717.jpeg)According to research, 83% of millennials choose to do business with companies that share their values. As a result, brands must publicly (and genuinely) endorse causes they believe in.

Taco Bell's foundation has been doing this for years, but they brought it to a whole new level by selling taco-themed NFT GIFs to fund the Live Más Scholarship.

 

All GIFs were gone within 30 minutes of placing their 25 NFTs (dubbed NFTacoBells) up for sale on Rarible. Each GIF had a starting bid price of $1. However, they all sold for thousands of dollars, with one going for $3,646.

Creating and selling NFTs was a wise move made by Taco Bell as it generated a lot of interest in mainstream and social media, which is always good for business.

Like Taco Bell, you can use NFTs to:

1.  Drive brand awareness

2.  Support a good cause

Both are powerful factors that can aid in the growth of your business.

2.  RTFKT Digital Sneakers

 

Looking for a way to make a name for yourself and disrupt the market?

NFTs can help you do that.

That's what happened when RTFKT, a relatively unknown Chinese virtual sneaker company, produced an NFT sneaker for the Chinese New Year and put it up for auction.

The sneaker sold for a whopping $28,000.

![](Images/image00718.jpeg)

 

That's very impressive for a brand that's only been around for two years, especially given that they sold a sneaker that can't even be touched, let alone worn. As impressive as this was, it was still way behind the $3 million they made from another NFT sneaker they designed with the 18-year-old artist, FEWOCiOUS.

While NFTs are still in their infancy, now is the best time for marketers to get on board. It's a great way to get people's attention and build a tribe of followers.

If you're a marketer looking for new ways to use NFT technology, you can take a cue from RTFKT. Create limited edition memorabilia to commemorate special occasions and holidays, and use them in your holiday marketing campaigns. You may give them away to the first X number of customers or sell them separately at an auction.

 

 

3.  Grimes Videos

 

Six million dollars in twenty minutes.

Grimes made that much from a collection of ten NFTs auctioned on Nifty Gateway.

 

 

People are interested in NFTs, and brands can capitalize on that interest to sell their goods.

For example, you can:

•  Partner with artists or auction sites to have your brand present at the auction.

•  Run a contest (to generate leads) with NFTs as the prize.

•  Create an NFT and auction it for charity.

 

Marketing is all about riding current trends and using your imagination to capitalize on the buzz around them to draw attention to your brand.

4.           The Launch of The Kings of Leon's Album "When You See Yourself."

 

The music industry has become extremely competitive due to a large number of musicians and bands available.

Building and maintaining a loyal fanbase isn't as easy as it once was.

The Kings of Leon devised a strategy to get around that.

They released their album "When You See Yourself" in the form of an NFT.

The Kings of Leon made history by becoming the first band to release an NFT version of an album.

More importantly, it put them in the hearts of their fans by enabling them to own a digital collectible. That's a fantastic way to increase brand loyalty.

Kings of Leon are used three different types of tokens for this first-of-its-kind album launch. One type offered live show perks, the second featured exclusive audiovisual art, while the third featured a special album package.

The album is available on all music platforms, but the NFT edition was only available for $50 on YellowHeart.

The sale of the NFTs was only open for 2 weeks, after which no more album tokens were produced. The tokens became a trade-able collectible as a result of this change.

 

 

5.  Beeple Artwork

 

Mike Winkelmann, who was virtually unknown in mainstream art circles, has become a legend.

He sold a JPG file for $69.3 million, making him the third most expensive living artist at the time of the auction.

The file is a piece of art sold as a non-fungible token.

 

 

A flurry of bids came in as the auction was about to close, forcing the two-week timed auction to be extended by 90 seconds.

What lessons can brands learn from this?

Be swift to adopt new concepts and innovations. With the competition becoming more fierce by the day, you must be willing to take chances and be disruptive to outperform.

6.  Nyan Cat GIF

The Nyan Cat GIF made a colorful impact on the digital scene a decade ago. Creator Chris Torres created an NFT version of the GIF that sold for over $500,000 on the crypto auction site foundation.

![](Images/image00719.jpeg)

That's right! A vintage animated GIF sold for more than half a million dollars.

However, Chris didn't stop there. He organized an auction in which classic memes were sold as NFTs. Bad Luck Brian, one of the memes, sold for over $34,000 on foundation.

What can brands learn from this?

The takeaway here is that consumers can pay for exceptional service. Take advantage of this by converting some of your most successful ads into NFTs. Make an event where you auction them off and make sure it is well-publicized.

Not only would this increase brand recognition, but it will also help you reach out to new tech markets.

The Future of NFTs

NFTs are still new, and their practical applications are still limited. However, people adore them and are willing to spend money on them. These are sure indicators that they are here to stay.

NFTs, including the blockchain technology that powers them, can play a major role in the future digital world. Non- fungible tokens have opened up new ways to engage with your audience and create unforgettable experiences for them, which is especially true for marketers.

Remember that most of the tools we use today (like social media) were once considered fads.

Now in today's world, we depend on them for so many things. NFTs can seem to be a fad right now, but they offer several advantages (such as transparency and security) that overcome the limitations of current technologies.

# CHAPTER 8:

 

# NFT VIRTUAL REAL ESTATE

 

 

 

What NFT Sales Mean for Digital Real Estate

What’s to stop virtual world real estate from increasing in value if NFT artworks can be worth millions?

In New York City, there is a row of tall, skinny, condominium buildings along the southern end of Central Park that has become known as “Billionaire’s Row.” The average apartment there sells for more than $37 million, with some going for more than $100 million and one going for a whopping $240 million.

While the buildings feature ultra-luxury finishes and breathtaking views, locals often discuss how ridiculous the prices seem. Perhaps more remarkable is that almost no one lives in these apartments; they are empty. The fact that these empty apartments sell for such high prices astounds people.

Janine Yorio is the Republic's real estate division and the Republic Realm NFT fund, which invests in digital real estate.

It turns out that humans are notoriously bad at valuing assets, especially when their value is exponentially higher than their actual utility. This explains why people are perplexed by Billionaire's Row prices or constantly surprised when art trades for large amounts of money.

People were understandably shocked when Christie's sold Beeple's digital art for $69 million. (Beeple is an artist who had never sold a piece of art for more than $100)

Although many people assume that asset prices are determined by their utility, in reality, asset values are determined by two factors: collective belief and transferability.

For instance, money has value only because people assume they will be able to trade it easier for goods and services in the future. Like currency, its value is determined by society's mutual consensus on its worth – and nothing else. The cost of the materials used to create art has no impact on its value, so most people cannot price it accurately.

Another virtual asset is a website domain name, which is simply a series of letters that exist only online. Several domain names have sold for over $10 million each. (Carinsurance.com, which sold for $49.7 million, was the most expensive domain name ever sold.) Yet, we've come to terms with the fact that such domain names trade for high prices due to their perceived rarity.

So, when a friend asked, "Why would I pay real money for fake land?" I explained that digital real estate is similar to NFT art and domain names. Since the marginal cost of creating a digital parcel of land is so low, its value is determined by its perceived scarcity rather than its actual utility.

Recently, digital real estate prices have risen faster than real estate prices (just ask artist Krista Kim, who sold an NFT of a

house for $500,000). Some may also argue that the prices are exorbitant. Meanwhile, some of the world's busiest streets, such as Madison Avenue and Broadway in New York and Oxford Street in London, are lined with vacant storefronts, heralding the so-called "retail apocalypse." Landlords cannot give away this space, not even for free.

 

 

## COLLECTIVE BELIEF IN THE VALUE OF VIRTUAL REAL ESTATE IS ALREADY GROWING.

People are always buying items even though stores sit empty. Real-world companies have tracked shoppers online in a never-ending quest for customers. Their next stop is the meta-verse, where their customers can socialize, window shop, and even purchase real-world items.

Although the number of users in meta-verses is only in the tens of thousands, when users start populating meta-verses in meaningful numbers, selling real-world items to people in those virtual worlds would become a very cost-effective marketing strategy. The convergence of real-world spending in virtual world environments is nothing new. For years, gamers have been purchasing “skins” and extra lives in video games. (Amazon is a fascinating case study on how e- commerce and brick-and-mortar shopping are being profoundly intertwined.)

It's a logical step for big companies to open virtual stores in virtual worlds, and they're already doing so.

Domino's, for example, has made it possible for people to order pizza from a store in the meta-verse Decentraland and have it delivered to their real-world address.

Also, Adidas and Karlie Kloss collaborated inside Decentraland, with attendees receiving a free (virtual) pair of Adidas shoes for their avatar to wear. People from all over the world attended this corporate-sponsored virtual gathering. Companies have realized that virtual events like these can be more cost-effective than real-world ones.

Imagine the possibilities if, instead of getting a store on every Main Street in America, Nike created one mind- blowing virtual shopping experience in the meta-verse that could sell to anybody, anywhere, at any time. Any company would eventually recognize the value of virtual stores in a meta-verse like Decentraland, just as they have all realized the value of having a website on the internet.

The outcome is self-fulfilling. More users will visit the meta- verses as the content becomes more compelling, attracting more corporate sponsorships.

So does it make sense that “fake” land trades at rates comparable to real-world values?

In 2014, Chanel paid $123.8 million for a nearly 4,000-square- foot retail space on Madison Avenue in New York City. This sale set records at $31,000 per square foot, and many people thought the price was absurd at the time.

However, suppose a virtual storefront can appeal to an infinite number of potential customers (millions more than walk down Madison Avenue in a year). In that case, virtual

land's value could inevitably trade at prices as perplexing as condos on Billionaire's Row or Beeple's art. The consensus on the importance of virtual real estate is increasing.

Furthermore, the Beeple sale has shown that a legitimizing force like Christie's can draw massive amounts of real-world wealth. As real-world businesses establish themselves in virtual worlds, they can raise property prices and encourage investment in new, community building projects, resulting in creating a new and valuable digital real estate ecosystem with credible players and substantial price tags.

The art world's rapid adoption of NFTs shows how rapidly these changes can occur. While it might seem irrational to spend $10,000 on a pixelated "piece of land," note that some digital NBA TopShot cards sold for $9 a pack in November have since sold for $200,000 each.

So we know why digital real estate may be valuable, but

why do we think it will continue to be valuable or appreciate? It's the same reason why people pay more for digital art and digital basketball cards than their real-world equivalents: transferability.

You've surely heard of refugees weaving gemstones into the hems of their clothes to conceal their wealth and pass it around as they emigrate. Crypto assets can be easily moved around. Not only is digital real estate becoming increasingly valuable, but its transferability can propel its value far beyond any perceived usefulness or scarcity value.

Unlike real-world real estate, digital real estate can be sold in minutes without the need for an attorney because its history

of ownership is logged on a self-contained decentralized blockchain ledger. Furthermore, digital basements and buildings never flood, and they never need a new roof.

The current escalating prices of NFTs and digital real estate are insignificant compared to what they might be worth once the rest of the world catches on. As a result, I believe the best virtual real estate parcels can appreciate faster than real-world real estate.

 

## Here Comes the Virtual Real Estate Boom

With NFTs on the rise and the real estate market being shaken by COVID-19, virtual worlds like Decentraland could be well-positioned to capitalize.

I've spent my entire professional career coming up with new ways to invest in real estate, and I've recently spent a lot of time in Decentraland. One of my colleagues has been urging me to investigate blockchain-based digital real estate for years, but it wasn't until recently that it all clicked for me.

Digital real estate has established itself as a legitimate asset class worthy of investor consideration and one that I believe will appreciate exponentially in the near future.

Because of its alignment with the increasingly growing crypto-investment universe, digital real estate does not only have the potential to deliver outsized returns, but it also appears to have the potential to become a viable store of wealth, similar to real-world real estate and real-world art. Digital real estate exists inside virtual worlds, each with its own "digital nation" and system of clearly defined,

irrevocable property rights. Purchasing land in virtual worlds today feels a lot like purchasing land in Manhattan in 1750\. It's also protected from the real-world real estate industry's COVID-induced uncertainty.

Janine Yorio is the head of real estate at Republic, an online investment platform for individual investors who recently launched Republic Realm, a digital real estate fund portfolio. She previously served as a portfolio manager for NorthStar Capital and as the head of real estate production at Standard Hotels.

In virtual worlds, players inhabit an avatar's body and communicate with the world, or “meta-verse." They can communicate with other users, earn cryptocurrency by playing games and gambling, purchase art in galleries, attend events and concerts, and various other activities. Cryptovoxels, Axie Infinity, Somnium Space, and The Sandbox (which have not yet launched) are among the other crypto-based virtual worlds. Still, Decentraland is the fastest-growing and most developed of them all.

 

## Why Decentraland?

Decentraland is a multiplayer role-playing game developed by two Argentine software engineers, Ari Meilich and Estaban Ordano. The world revolves around a plaza known as Genesis City. Unlike most video games, it has no predetermined goal other than to evolve into a virtual world created and controlled by its users. It shares similarities with

early virtual games like Second Life and SimCity, and recent multiplayer games like Fortnite and Minecraft.

The fact that Decentraland's economy is founded on a blockchain is what distinguishes it. All parcels (called "LAND" in the game) except for plazas and roads can be purchased, sold, and built by game users using "MANA," Decentraland's crypto-token. (MANA's market capitalization is around $225 million, up more than fivefold since its inception in 2017.)

As of February 2021, MANA prices had risen 321 percent in the previous year.

LAND ownership is a non-fungible token (NFT) registered on the Ethereum blockchain using the ERC-721 standard (the same as CryptoKitties), making it easily transferable and less susceptible to fraud. The total number of LAND parcels that will ever be minted has been set at 90,061 by the game's developers. LAND parcels are non-fungible Since each parcel has a unique set of (x,y) coordinates.

There's also a secondary market for buying and selling LAND, as well as a supporting an economy of contractors willing to design and build on these virtual parcels for very real-world prices. People are willing to invest considerable amounts of money in virtual real estate because they believe it is scarce and valuable. As a result, among early adopters, owning one has become something of a status symbol.

Beyond status, ownership signifies something a little more – a contribution to the Decentraland community's fabric. How players in Decentraland interact with your LAND is heavily influenced by where and how you develop your parcel. They become places for people to interact, explore, build, and socialize, simulating real-life social interactions while also increasing the LAND value.

As a result, Decentraland real estate has seen a multi-year period of price appreciation. In 2017, the year Decentraland was introduced, LAND parcels sold for about $100 per parcel. Estate 331, a part of the “Genesis Plaza” estate, sold for around $80,000 in 2019, making it the year's second-most expensive non-fungible token (NFT). A few months ago, the price of an undeveloped plot of land increased to about 8,000 MANA (approximately $1,400), a 14-fold increase in just 3 years. There have been over 50,000 secondary LAND sales totaling $30+ million at an average price of $560 since the game's launch, so these data points aren't outliers. The overall value of all of the LAND is currently about $100 million – and rising.

Investing in digital real estate has all of the benefits of derivative trades (asymmetric reward-risk) without the bad (recourse margin/debt). It's also an uncorrelated asset class that helps to offset market volatility. Although it was once thought that cryptocurrencies (particularly BTC) behaved similarly to digital gold, backtesting has shown that crypto- assets behave differently from gold, bonds, or equity. It's a fantastic tool for portfolio diversification.

There are many reasons why I think digital real estate will continue to rise in value.

Bitcoin, an asset that trades entirely outside of the traditional banking system, was the best-performing asset of the last decade, knocking out Amazon and Apple stock. Cryptocurrency, which was once thought to be a gimmick for gamers and coders, has now become so mainstream that everyone with a smartphone can purchase and sell it, and major financial institutions are finally following suit.

# CHAPTER 9:

 

# THE RISE OF NFTS

 

 

Investment in virtual real estate occurs through the exchange and purchase of non-fungible tokens, a special type of cryptographic token representing something unique; non-fungible tokens are therefore not mutually interchangeable. This is in comparison to the fungibility of cryptocurrencies, including bitcoin. Each NFT is unique, indivisible, and non-interchangeable.

Digital real estate NFTs are exchangeable on NFT marketplaces through transactions that are much more transparent and streamlined than real-world real estate transactions. Still, they are not as liquid as other cryptocurrencies that trade on larger exchanges.

Ownership in virtual real estate is registered on a decentralized ledger through an NFT, rather than a conventional deed or title, difficult to transfer. Holders retain ownership of their digital assets indefinitely, even if Decentraland is shut down or abandoned by the developers.

Evidence of acceptance and adoption of NFTs as a proxy for ownership can be best demonstrated by the depth of the market and the magnitude of some recent transactions, including a $1.5 million sale of property in the metaverse Axie Infinity.

In this environment of low-interest rates and high inflation, the quest for resilient assets will continue, making crypto assets seem less volatile than other real-world asset types.

I'm worried about the real-world real estate market. Retail, office, and hospitality rents and occupancy have plummeted, and their future outlook remains bleak. Single-

family apartments and homes seem immune to the pandemic, but how much higher can their prices rise before they become unaffordable in the face of stagnant wages?

Real estate remains one of the world's most valuable stores of capital and inflation hedges, but even the most adamant proponents are debating how to value real estate today.

Early versions of Decentraland were clumsy, but the game has vastly improved since its launch, and players have noticed. The game's landowners have spent a lot of time and money creating intricate architectural structures, wearables, and games. The game's developers are constantly adding new functionality. They work with unparalleled transparency, providing the community with a complete picture of what rollouts, features, and improvements to expect for Decentraland's future on a publicly accessible Trello board.

The in-game discussion boards, as well as those on Discord and Reddit, are extremely active. The secondary market for in-game purchases such as land and wearables appears to be growing in liquidity and volume. Every time I

revisit Decentraland, I notice more users and interactivity. At the same time, in-game asset prices are rising.

Despite this, Decentraland remains sparsely populated, with most LAND remaining undeveloped. Some of the sparseness is because the game was designed to satisfy a massive user base that has yet to materialize, and as a result,

it is played through several servers. This system was inspired by games like Minecraft and Fortnite, which have hundreds of thousands of players at any given time and thus need many "servers" to avoid feeling overcrowded. As a result, a player could only encounter a few other players in a typical visit to Decentraland.

This reality might seem disturbing to some. (Where are all the people?) To me, it seems to be a fantastic opportunity to get in early, at the beginning of a new utopia for self- proclaimed misfits.

## Virtual socialization is inevitable

Virtual worlds aren't new. Both Eve Online and Second Life were designed for adults, introduced in 2003, and attracted millions of users at their peak. Players built complex economies within those games. However, both games peaked in popularity when the real world was still heavily reliant on real-life interaction and have since faded in favor of the modern crypto-based upstarts. Those games were just a bit too early.

Since then, human interaction and socialization have largely become virtual, with most people transferring their social and business interactions to their phones or laptop. If there was ever a time when people would choose to socialize on screens rather than in person, it is now when a global pandemic has forced us all online. These new habits would become lasting behavioral and cultural shifts, altering our perceptions of what is normal and acceptable in the long run.

There are many instances of this shift toward less real-life interaction, such as the emergence of Clubhouse, a mix of podcasts and a 1980s party line and is one of the fastest- growing startups of 2020. It's a completely virtual – and audio – a social experience that's exploding in popularity.

When we invest, especially in real estate, we must try to forecast the future – a 10 or more years away. Today's children will be young adults in ten years, and their tastes will determine what becomes mainstream and normative.

My children are fully addicted to virtual-world video games, especially Minecraft, the best-selling video game of all time. (For instance, if I ask my kids if they'd rather play with their friends or play Minecraft, they'll always choose the latter.) Their Minecraft addiction – and that of millions of other school-aged children have been fueled by nearly a year of online education, which has culminated in excessive amounts of screen time and access to video games.

Minecraft and other related games are influencing how kids communicate with technology and each other. Decentraland and other adults crypto-focused virtual worlds aim at players who have outgrown children's games and are looking for deeper human interactions based on socializing and transacting rather than treasure hunting and enemy slaughter. The adult games' user interface (UX) follows the same conventions as the children's games.

 

 

## VIRTUAL REAL ESTATE: NEW PLAYERS, NEW RULES

Ailin Graef, better known as Anshe Chung, started collecting virtual real estate in Second Life in 2004. She began with less than $10 and rose to prominence as the first avatar to amass a net worth of more than $1 million through business dealings conducted entirely inside the virtual world. The mythology surrounding Anshe Chung is responsible for today's enthusiasm for virtual real estate and NFT speculation.

Learning the latest rules is the secret to speculating on land in the virtual world. The old rules, which favored the already wealthy, are no longer valid. The old real estate adage "location, location, location" means that values

are determined by their visibility and proximity. Still, in virtual worlds, players may teleport to new locations using cartesian coordinates, so foot traffic and visibility are less important.

What matters in the virtual world is bringing life and humanity to something pixelated and flat, drawing players somewhere, and encouraging them to return and interact. (These settlements are also referred to as “clusters of content.”) In the virtual world, design and ingenuity matter far more than budget and location.

The developers of Decentraland have taken a very careful and light-handed approach to construct the game's foundational layers. New projects and collaborations are already underway to transform this virtual world into a sophisticated and interactive alternate reality – one that will likely become as popular and addictive as Instagram and Facebook are today.

There will not be a single winner in Decentraland. And that is why it will succeed. By leveling the playing field. Decentraland has set itself up to be a winner in the quest to create the ideal virtual world – a utopia.

For all of these reasons, I am convinced that digital real estate is a viable investment option. While this asset class is still highly risky and a full loss of principal is a probable outcome, it may be wise for risk-tolerant investors to consider allocating a small portion of their alternative investment portfolio to this emerging asset class.

Furthermore, participants in this virtual economy will discover that it is an exciting time to be a part of it.

Perhaps the most jaded of us would be enticed by the prospect of moving to a new world and reinventing themselves. When coupled with colorful graphics, music, art, and new friends from around the world, it’s easy to understand why Decentraland is becoming more popular.

Early adopters of virtual real estate will get at still-affordable prices compared to real-world markets. Those who invest early and hold for the long term would benefit, much like early movers in fast-growing areas in the real world.

# CHAPTER 10:

 

# HOW NFTS BECAME ART, AND EVERYTHING BECAME AN NFT

 

 

 

Pascal Boyart is a Parisian street artist. He painted a mural inspired by Eugène Delacroix's masterwork "Liberty Leading the People" in a working-class neighborhood in January 2019. He gave it a modern spin. Boyart replaced the nineteenth century French rebels (those who toppled King Charles X) with the “yellow-jacket” protesters, who were either fighting for justice or inciting riots and burning the city down, depending on who you ask. The French government was not happy. They soon painted over Boyart's work with a neutral gray, blotting his message from existence.

“Today, the physical mural does not exist”. Boyart tells me from Paris. “This gives me a lot of pleasure.”

Boyart created a non-fungible token (NFT) version of the painting, divided into 100 parts and sold for 0.5 ETH each. He'd sell a lot more NFTs. Boyart had trouble monetizing his street art in the past – graffiti is difficult to sell, and few street artists make Banksy-money – but NFTs gave him a method to sell his work directly to his fans. And it's suddenly become pretty profitable. On Feb. 26, his NFT "Contemplations of the Red Jester" sold for 75 ETH, or

$112,000 at the time, making it his largest sale to date.

Boyart has company. NFT sales are soaring, punctuated by a $6.6 million, 10-second video created by Beeple. Beeple's crypto-works are presently being auctioned at Christie's.

(For those who aren't familiar with Beeple, he is a well- known digital artist, and Christie's has been around since before the United States Declaration of Independence.)

It's nearly impossible to keep up with the latest NFT developments. Here are a few examples: Grimes sold an NFT bundle for $6 million; an NFT tweet (a tweet!) from Mark Cuban sold for $952; The cartoon frog HomerPepe sold for $320,000; Lindsay Lohan now mints NFTs; Shawn Mendes hawks NFT guitars; people are selling genesis tweets; and possibly by the time you finish reading this paragraph, perhaps we will get news that President Biden is dropping stimulus checks through NFTs.

This is no longer a tiny niche. According to research by NonFungible, over $250 million of NFT volume was traded in 2020, and that doesn't include the recent spike. It also doesn't include the $230 million spent on NBA Top Shot, a digital "moment" (basically brief video highlights) that was so popular that its developers were overwhelmed by a 200,000-person queue.

Want to buy an NFT of a LeBron dunk? One of them recently sold for $208,000. Mark Cuban is all over it, and it's all happening in a flash. He told CoinDesk less than two months ago that he was exploring NFTs (which was breaking news at the time), and now, according to USA Today, he believes NFTs “could evolve into a top-three revenue stream for the NBA over the next ten years.”

NFTs can be music. NFTs can be art. NFTs can be real estate, sports, collectibles, fantasy football, internet ephemera, gaming, and just about everything shy of thoughts. (It won’t be long.) I’m waiting for the first wedding proposal NFT. Is it possible to mint NFTs as NFTs? A recent discussion on Clubhouse “NFT Performance Art” included a conversation about NFTs and the soul. I was only there for a few minutes, but it was enough time for someone to inquire, “Is there a way to play rock-paper-scissors with NFTs?”

This raises a few questions. What sparked the NFT mania? Why now? Is this just another fad in the crypto world - a bubble destined to burst, or do NFTs provide genuine value to the collector or the creator? After all, most people go to YouTube to watch highlights of LeBron throwing tomahawks for free. Why are we paying millions of dollars for a virtual bucket of cartoon frogs?

 

 

## PANDEMIC EFFECT

Let's start with the fundamentals: NFTs are easy to visualize. They are concrete. A song, a piece of art, or even a digital home in a "metaverse" like Decentraland - Non-crypto people may easily understand these things, which is one reason for the crossover appeal.

The only awkward part of explaining NFTs is the name. The cumbersome acronym "Non-Fungible Token" causes confusion when you explain that an NFT is essentially just something digital that can't be copied or falsified thanks to blockchain technology.

Much of crypto is abstract, complicated, and tangled up in a tricky web of tech and coding and economic theory. Make an effort to explain "yield farming" to your grandparents. NFTs are a bridge to more enjoyable universes. They're also a means to reach out to a large number of new audiences.

“A lot of people are uninterested in decentralized finance... But the average person might like basketball,” says Mason Nystrom, a Messari research analyst who specializes in NFTs. “This allows you to reach out to a whole new group of people who might not have been previously interested in crypto.”

According to Nystrom, decentralized finance (DeFi) is estimated to have between 1 million and 2 million users. That's not small. However, it is nothing compared to the 42.7 million people who follow the NBA on Facebook, many of whom will be interested in NBA Top Shot. O as Zack Seward put it, "it’s captivating a lot of curiosity outside of usual crypto-dork circles." And why is this happening now, as opposed to 2018 or 2017, when NFTs were originally introduced? Five quick theories:

1) The on-ramps and marketplaces for NFTs – such as NiftyGateway, Sorare, OpenSea, Mintbase, and SuperRare – are significantly more intuitive and user-friendly than they were in 2017.

2) Investors have ETH burning a hole in their pockets due to the crypto bull run.

3) During the coronavirus outbreak, both collectors and creators are stuck at home and looking for something to do.

4) The emergence of NFTs coincides with a larger increase in real-world collectibles (such as the resurgence of baseball cards).

5)           Influencers like Cuban have entered the space, generating a virtuous cycle that pumps additional interest.

However, there could be a more powerful factor at work. “I believe that everyone should be looking at some of the larger trends that are happening with humanity,” says the Hong Kong-based pseudonymous "Whale Shark," who claims to be the world's second-largest NFT collector with over 210,000 pieces.

“A growing number of people spend their time on their phones or online... “We are transitioning from a physical to a digital lifestyle,” says Shark. Most of us look at two, three, or four screens every day, especially during a pandemic.

According to Whale, it's only natural that when we want to own things, we're getting more comfortable owing them in our digital lives, which are increasingly becoming our lives. Then he introduces a new dynamic: Even before NFTs, the younger generation of collectors had become accustomed to investing real money in digital-only ecosystems such as Fortnite. This is an old hat for them, which is why the mega- collector says, "I think of NFTs as a no-brainer."

 

 

## OWN IT

The potential of blockchain technology to "revolutionize ownership" has long sounded like puffery, yet NFTs are doing just that. Consider Eve Sussman, a prominent mixed- media artist (sculpture, photography, film) whose work has been shown at the Museum of Modern Art and the Smithsonian Institution.

She created a 12-minute video called "89 seconds at Alcazár" for the Whitney Biennial in 2004, reimagining a moment from Diego Velásquez's 1656 classic "Las Meninas." Critics swooned. It was dubbed a "standout" by New York magazine, a work so revelatory that "you will sense what composition, in art, entails."

Sussman’s video found a second life with NFTs. Snark was the firm with which she worked. Art - a blockchain-savvy agency that collaborates with artists to create innovative formats to carve the video into a 2,304-piece grid. As an NFT, collectors could buy a single tiny fragment. Each of these "atoms" is only 20 by 20 pixels and can be watched as a tiny film. They called the new form “89 Seconds Atomized.”

So far, this appears to be similar to Boyart's method of dividing his street art (the yellow vest mural) into 100 pieces. They didn't stop at fractional ownership, either. They added a fascinating twist. If you buy one of Sussman's 2,304 atoms, you can only see one little piece of his work, which doesn't make much sense out of context.

Then there's the blockchain. You may “request a loan” from all of the other NFT-owners of “89 Seconds Atomized” thanks to the magic of smart contracts (code in NFTs that defines ownership), and when they loan you all of their pixels, you may recreate the piece in all of its original glory. Misha Libman, a co-founder of Snark. Art, who worked on the partnership, says, "We were thinking, what could ownership look like in the digital sense?"

“You have fairly pricey artwork that most people cannot afford. But if you fracture it and sell each piece for 100 USD, you give collectors the chance to be a member of this community and own a piece of a famous work of art while also being an active participant.”

NFTs come in various shapes and sizes. They can be only a replica of a physical object, but they can also be an improvement. They can unlock new creative

possibilities. This is possibly best demonstrated through art, such as Boyart's street murals. Most paintings are static. This is something that every Kindergartener understands. Paint is immobile. Boyart, on the other hand, uses his NFTs to add motion, audio and augmented reality to the images.

Boyart animated the NFT in "Raft of the Medusa" so that the raft bobs and sways in the water. Dollar banknotes (or euros) float ominously to the floor in “Contemplations of the Red Jester” as the Jester slumps in his chair, desolate.

Tricks like this have long been possible with digital art; however, none of those earlier forms had solved digital scarcity. NFTs have solved that puzzle. Smart contracts can also allow the artist to program alterations to the sculpture in the future. Boyart, for example, is currently developing an NFT whose layers will alter over time. NFT art may be programmed to display various pictures triggered by real- world events such as the weather, Christmas morning, or the outcome of an election — all of these are now possible.

NFTs are luring in artists from outside the blockchain world. Take, for example, Elizabeth Meggs, an oil painter based in Brooklyn, New York. Meggs is a friend of mine,

and when she emailed me out of the blue asking about NFTs,

I was startled. She has a weekly Zoom chat with other artists from her studio, and they are constantly discussing NFTs.

“The first unspoken rule of talking about art on Clubhouse is that all conversations inevitably lead to obsessing over NFTs,” says Marion Maneker on ArtNews. Understandably, artists would be early adopters. According to Meggs, “artists are often onto beautiful things before the general public,” such as “renting studios in neighborhoods before the neighborhoods become hot.”

87 percent of major American museum collections are from men – 85 percent from white men. In theory, NFTs have the potential to level the field.

Meggs is enthusiastic about making her own NFTs, exploring the creative possibilities they provide, and seeing how they may help make the art world more inclusive - especially for women and people of color. They establish direct contact between the author and the collector, bypassing the intermediaries who have long demonstrated bias.

“I want to make artwork," says Meggs, "I don't want to spend my entire life and psyche fighting a deeply prejudiced system where I have to rely on key-holders – at very biased arts institutions – to open many doors for credibility and success. Rather than slamming doors, NFTs feel like a fantastic new universe full of possibility,” she explains.

Meggs isn't naive. She is well aware that crypto is just as male-dominated as the art world. She is, nonetheless, optimistic. “I'm urging all of the female artists I know to start NFTs right away,” she says. “This is an opportunity!” (Meggs also mentioned in a follow-up email that many artists are concerned about the environmental implications of NFTs, which have already been discussed in this book .)

Another characteristic of NFTs that attracts vandalism artists is the ability to collect royalties. That is a gap in today’s world. Assume you're a young artist who sells a $1,000 oil painting. You have a feeling it's worth more, but you have to pay rent. Then you become popular. Your art is sold for $30 million thirty years later. Your royalties in the current world? Nothing. (It’s different overseas. “Droit de suite, aka Artists Resale Right, provides a resale fee to artists or their heirs... and is prevalent in many nations around the world, particularly in Europe. But, regrettably, in America, we only have a first-sale policy,” says Meggs.)

With NFTs, the royalty may be coded into the piece through a smart contract, ensuring that you receive a portion of the proceeds every time your painting is sold in perpetuity.

Boyart received a 5% cut when his "Contemplations of the Red Jester" sold for 75 ETH - from one art collector to another.

The same concepts apply to music. Just ask “Vandal,” a Canadian hip-hop artist and producer. He dropped a track in 2017, called “Rap Crypto,” that’s something of a time capsule from the last bull run, with lyrics like “Rap Crypto Y’all Know Bitcoin … See I’m Vandal, the Token Wrapper … Follow me as I go, Rambo, to the moon in my brand new Lambo … Could it be that I’m Satoshi Nakamoto?”

Vandal shrugged CryptoKitties off at first, thinking, "What the hell is this, collecting cats?" But then he saw the bigger picture. He began investigating audio NFTs, became captivated by DAOs (decentralized autonomous organizations), and eventually founded DAO Records, which has enlisted the help of 100 musicians and published around 50 audio NFTs. (Vandal is quick to point out that he isn't the first artist to release music-based NFTs, referring to Connie Digital as "the OG of audio NFTs.")

For Vandal, audio NFTs aren't just a brilliant gimmick, a fad, or a clever way to generate money. “The present music industry model is broken,” a problem that extends back to the Napster era. “Then Apple came along and decided that a 99-cent MP3 was worth it. Who gave them the authority to determine the value of music? Music lost a lot of value as a result of that.” Streaming on YouTube or Spotify isn't much better, as "they pay so little to the artists while taking so much." There is no sharing. There is no interaction with the fans," he says.

According to Vandal, NFTs solve all of these problems. Artists are free to price their music however they choose. “The NFT provides a direct link to the fan... You are free to offer them whatever you want.” It's a way to foster loyalty, strengthen the community, and wean oneself from the platforms (and labels) over which you have no control. This explains why artists like Kings of Leon, who recently announced plans to release an album as an NFT, are so interested in them. You can combine NFTs with real-world perks. According to Rolling Stone, the most exclusive NFTs would feature a "golden ticket," giving token users "four front-row seats to any Kings of Leon show during each tour for life.”

It's so thrilling; it liberates people.

Kings of Leon is not the only band considering NFT ticketing. "It's a great use case," says Carolin Wend, co- founder of Mintbase, a Lisbon-based NFT platform. She notes that ticket-based NFTs (TNFTs?) allow event organizers, like a music festival, to distribute revenue among partners simply.

Assume you're a festival promoter. You can tell the DJ, "you will get 5 percent of every ticket sold," and then the reggae band will get 5%. The landlord receives 10 percent, and so on. Payments would be immediate, transparent, and verifiable, and confirmed on a blockchain. “It ensures that employees working in the creative economy are paid fairly.”

Coachella and Ultra are unlikely to embrace the idea of NFT tickets anytime soon, just as NFTs are unlikely to fully "disrupt" the traditional art world anytime soon. Misha Libman of Snark. Art estimates that the art market itself is roughly $60 billion in annual sales. “Presently, digital art is less than half of that,” he says. “It’s almost non-existent in the traditional market.”

I spoke with a man who embodies tradition. Nicholas Lowry is the principal auctioneer at Swann Auction Galleries, New York's oldest indigenous auction house. He is known for his flamboyant plaid suits and handlebar mustache.

“The interesting thing is, NFTs popped up on my radar from six separate locations all at once,” says Lowry. Everyone started telling him about NFTs all of a sudden. They kept sending him articles. “If this were a marketing campaign, or an ad campaign for a vehicle or a clothes brand, they would have somehow won the jackpot,” he says, clearly amused. “Holy s\*\*t, the whole world is talking about this.”

So is he ready to jump on the trend? Lowry pauses. "We aren't a cutting-edge technology company." “I don't want to be the first person to do it, but I also don't want to be the last.”

He's in a state of "wait and see." Maybe it's the future, but it could just be a passing trend. “When it’s not the Elon Musks who are buying the stuff, we will see if it still has legs.”

 

## My very first NFT

One reason for the market's development is that it's now easy to whip up your own NFTs. “Today, anyone can picture their plant, mint it, and then offer it on an exchange and try to sell it,” Libman explains.

So I decided to give it a shot.

I recently relocated into a new place in Denver; Because I'm a single guy, I didn't have anything to hang on the walls. So I chose some of my greatest vacation images to enlarge and frame, and I chose a 30” by 50” shot of a glacier from Iceland to anchor my largest wall.

Maybe I could sell this image as an NFT? If I thought it has enough real-life value to hang as a piece of art on a massive wall, maybe others would feel the same way? I also wanted to have a firsthand look at how this worked.

I instantly created an OpenSea account. The on-boarding process is simple and quick. When asked to input a starting price for an auction, the lowest the site will let me enter is

$540 or 0.4 ETH at the time, an absurdly high sum that I found funny. (You can either list it for a lower fixed price or start an auction, with this being the lowest starting point for the auction. It’s also entirely possible that I did it wrong.) But, hey, $540 for this NFT pic, why not? So much of cryptocurrency is based on the idea that if people believe something has value, it does. Even Dogecoin now has value. Perhaps someone would think my pic is worth 0.4 ETH, so they'd buy it, and voila, it would?

Soaring gas fees are a widespread problem in space.

There was a minor snag before I publish my ad, I'll have to pay OpenSea a one-time gas fee to get the Ethereum smart contract set up for all of my future sales. This fee was $91 at the time. Soaring gas fees are a widespread issue in the space, and it's one reason why some NFT platforms are switching

to various blockchains (NBA Top Shot uses Flow, for example).

Mintbase is migrating to a blockchain platform called NEAR because “normal people would never spend $50 to mint an NFT,” according to Wend. “I think Ethereum has been a terrific proving ground for a lot of individuals, but it's not the future,” she says. “It's just too expensive and bloated.” (Of course, Ethereum enthusiasts are looking forward to Ethereum 2.0.)

My photo posting has suddenly turned into a costly bit of fact-checking, but I completed the transaction anyway.

When my new NFT is minted, I keep an eye on the stream of fresh OpenSea listings for it to appear. They appear every second, an assembly line of pixelated trinkets, like crypto toys manufactured by Satoshi's little elves. It gives you a real-time glimpse into how quickly this space is expanding.

At least one thing the NFT marketplace was right about was the value of my crappy photo, which was nowhere near 0.4 ETH. It didn’t sell in the auction. So I re-listed it for 0.01 ETH ($16). It has still not sold as of the time of publication.

 

## STATUS SYMBOLS

Let move our attention to the buyers. Why are people paying thousands of dollars for low-res art that isn't as advanced as Pac-Man? I had imagined there could be two reasons: People either enjoyed digital art for its aesthetic pleasure or because they believed they would make money from it.

Then I found out about a third possibility. I chatted with Jamie Burke, the CEO of Outlier Ventures, a blockchain investment firm based in London that offers venture capital, accelerator programs, and other services. Burke is also an avid collector of NFTs and is well-versed in the field. He created the “100XARt” Discord channel, a who's who of the biggest NFT collectors, and they collaborated to construct an art district in Decentraland. Burke’s theory on why people buy NFTs? The feeling of belonging to a community.

According to Burke, NFTs can be thought of as a form of social currency. In certain crypto circles, they are proof that you belong. When Burke first saw the early extremely pixilated NFT art, such as CryptoPunks, he didn't like the aesthetic. He thought it was excessively self-referential. “This is not something I would want on my wall,” he said to himself.

After speaking with several collectors in the community, he realized the NFTs had little to do with the content. What matters is the mere fact of ownership. “To own a CryptoPunk is to claim that you were into the initial form of NFTs and that you understood \[it\] before the rest of the world.” It's a digital badge of honor, in a literal sense.

This reminds Burke of why people used to buy LP albums back in the day. Sure, the warm and crackly romance of the sound may have enticed you to purchase the album, but you also purchased it as a treasured artifact. And the artifact gave you status within your record-loving community.

“Consider what the internet did. “All of that was destroyed by digital music,” he explains. “Music became a commodity that could be streamed and consumed. The idea of owning music has vanished.” While NFTs are a revolutionary new technology, they also tap into something primal and old, what Burke refers to as "a real human longing."

Spending money on NFTs, on the other hand, is a way to show off your crypto wealth. Just ask the guy (and it's almost likely a person) on Twitter who goes by the handle "GMoney.ETH." This person paid 140 ETH (about $150,000 at the time) for a 24 X 24 pixelated CryptoPunk, one of the first and most recognizable forms of crypto art. They have the appearance of Atari graphics from 1983. (For those interested in the origins of CryptoPunks, you will find a fascinating deep dive from BreakerMag and Jessica Klein.)

GMoney explains on Twitter, after spending more time in the online crypto community, he learned that "it's almost like being a member of some elite club, being a CryptoPunk owner." Then he gets to the quick of it: “When someone buys a Rolex in the real world, they don't spend thousands of dollars because of the watch's utility value." A $5 watch could perform the same function. It is to ‘flex’ their status.

GMoney purchased his version of a Rolex, but unlike a flashy watch which could be a cheap knockoff - the authenticity of his CryptoPunk can be validated on the blockchain. On the other hand, it's a near-certainty that a Rolex will hold its value in a decade.

Even if the speculative bubble bursts, it seems reasonable to claim that NFTs offer something of value - even if that value is purely emotional - to both collectors like Burke and artists like Boyart. They can be used to create, play, organize, communicate, and view beauty. “When you look at the 2017 bull run, what drove it was media news stories about people getting rich. That was one-dimensional. It fizzled out,” says Burke. People left when they stopped making money. "With NFTs, it’s not just about the money. It is much more sustainable," he says.

Of course, others may scoff and claim that most of these NFTs - the pixelated frogs, memes, and crypto irony – are simply "not art." That may be true. However, as NFT advocates like to point out, the same thing was said of Andy Warhol's tomato soup can.

# CHAPTER 11:

 

# NFT, REAL ESTATE AND AMAZING VIRTUAL WORLDS (DECENTRALAND, THE SANDBOX, SUPERWORLD…)

 

 

 

What NFT Sales Mean for Digital Real Estate

What is to stop virtual world real estate from increasing in value if NFT artworks can be worth millions?

In New York City, there is a row of tall, skinny, condominium buildings along the southern end of Central Park that has become known as “Billionaire’s Row.” The average apartment there sells for more than $37 million, with some going for more than $100 million and one going for a whopping $240 million.

While the buildings feature ultra-luxury finishes and breathtaking views, locals often discuss how ridiculous the prices seem. Perhaps more remarkable is that almost no one lives in these apartments; they are empty. The fact that these empty apartments sell for such high prices astounds people.

Janine Yorio is the Republic's real estate division and the Republic Realm NFT fund, which invests in digital real estate.

It turns out that humans are notoriously bad at valuing assets, especially when their value is exponentially higher than their actual utility. This explains why people are perplexed by Billionaire's Row prices or constantly surprised when art trades for large amounts of money.

People were understandably shocked when Christie's sold Beeple's digital art for $69 million last week. (Beeple is an artist who had never sold a piece of art for more than $100)

Although many people assume that asset prices are determined by their utility, in reality, asset values are determined by two factors: collective belief and transferability.

For instance, money has value only because people assume they will be able to trade it easier for goods and services in the future. Like currency, its value is determined by society's mutual consensus on its worth – and nothing else. The cost of the materials used to create art has no impact on its value, so most people cannot price it accurately.

Another virtual asset is a website domain name, which is simply a series of letters that exist only online. Several domain names have sold for over $10 million each. (Carinsurance.com, which sold for $49.7 million, was the most expensive domain name ever sold.) Yet, we've come to terms with the fact that such domain names trade for high prices due to their perceived rarity.

So, when a friend asked, "Why would I pay real money for fake land?" I explained that digital real estate is similar to NFT art and domain names. Since the marginal cost of creating a digital parcel of land is so low, its value is determined by its perceived scarcity rather than its actual utility.

Recently, digital real estate prices have risen faster than real estate prices (just ask artist Krista Kim, who sold an NFT of a house for $500,000). Some may also argue that the prices are exorbitant. Meanwhile, some of the world's busiest streets, such as Madison Avenue and Broadway in New York and Oxford Street in London, are lined with vacant storefronts, heralding the so-called "retail apocalypse." Landlords cannot give away this space, not even for free.

 

## COLLECTIVE BELIEF IN THE VALUE AND WORTH OF VIRTUAL REAL ESTATE IS ALREADY EXPANDING.

People are always buying items even though stores sit empty. Real-world companies have tracked shoppers online in a never-ending quest for customers. Their next stop is the metaverse, where their customers can socialize, window shop, and even purchase real-world items.

Although the number of users in metaverses is only in the tens of thousands, when users start populating metaverses in meaningful numbers, selling real-world items to people in those virtual worlds would become a very cost-effective marketing strategy. The convergence of real-world spending in virtual world environments is nothing new. For years, gamers have been purchasing “skins” and extra lives in video games. (Amazon is a fascinating case study on how e- commerce and brick-and-mortar shopping are being profoundly intertwined.)

It's a logical step for big companies to open virtual stores in virtual worlds, and they're already doing so.

Domino's, for example, has made it possible for people to order pizza from a store in the metaverse Decentraland and have it delivered to their real-world address.

Also, Adidas and Karlie Kloss collaborated inside Decentraland, with attendees receiving a free (virtual) pair of Adidas shoes for their avatar to wear. People from all over the world attended this corporate-sponsored virtual gathering. Companies have realized that virtual events like these can be more cost-effective than real-world ones.

Imagine the possibilities if, instead of getting a store on every Main Street in America, Nike created one mind- blowing virtual shopping experience in the metaverse that could sell to anybody, anywhere, at any time. Any company would eventually recognize the value of virtual stores in a metaverse like Decentraland, just as they have all realized the value of having a website on the internet.

The outcome is self-fulfilling. More users will visit the metaverses as the content becomes more compelling, attracting more corporate sponsorships.

So does it make sense that “fake” land trades at rates comparable to real-world values?

In 2014, Chanel paid $123.8 million for a nearly 4,000-square- foot retail space on Madison Avenue in New York City. This sale set records at $31,000 per square foot, and many people thought the price was absurd at the time.

However, suppose a virtual storefront can appeal to an infinite number of potential customers (millions more than walk down Madison Avenue in a year). In that case, virtual land's value could inevitably trade at prices as perplexing as Condos on Billionaire's Row or Beeple's art. The consensus on the importance of virtual real estate is increasing.

Furthermore, the Beeple sale has shown that a legitimizing force like Christie's can draw massive amounts of real-world wealth. As real-world businesses establish themselves in virtual worlds, they can raise property prices and encourage investment in new, community-building projects, resulting in creating a new and valuable digital real estate ecosystem with credible players and substantial price tags.

The art world's rapid adoption of NFTs shows how rapidly these changes can occur. While it might seem irrational to spend $10,000 on a pixelated "piece of land," note that some digital NBA TopShot cards sold for $9 a pack in November have since sold for $200,000 each.

So we know why digital real estate may be valuable, but

why do we think it will continue to be valuable or appreciate? It's the same reason why people pay more for digital art and digital basketball cards than their real-world equivalents: transferability.

You've surely heard of refugees weaving gemstones into the hems of their clothes to conceal their wealth and pass it around as they emigrate. Crypto assets can be easily moved around. Not only is digital real estate becoming increasingly valuable, but its transferability can propel its value far beyond any perceived usefulness or scarcity value.

Unlike real-world real estate, digital real estate can be sold in minutes without the need for an attorney because its history of ownership is logged on a self-contained decentralized blockchain ledger. Furthermore, digital basements and buildings never flood, and they never need a new roof.

The current escalating prices of NFTs and digital real estate are insignificant compared to what they might be worth once the rest of the world catches on. As a result, I believe the best virtual real estate parcels can appreciate faster than real-world real estate.

 

 

## Here Comes the Virtual Real Estate Boom

With NFTs on the rise and the real estate market being shaken by COVID-19, virtual worlds like Decentraland could be well-positioned to capitalize.

I've spent my entire professional career coming up with new ways to invest in real estate, and I've recently spent a lot of time in Decentraland. One of my colleagues has been urging me to investigate blockchain-based digital real estate for years, but it wasn't until recently that it all clicked for me.

Digital real estate has established itself as a legitimate asset class worthy of investor consideration and one that I believe will appreciate exponentially in the near future.

Because of its alignment with the increasingly growing crypto-investment universe, digital real estate does not only have the potential to deliver outsized returns, but it also appears to have the potential to become a viable store of wealth, similar to real-world real estate and real-world art. Digital real estate exists inside virtual worlds, each with its own "digital nation" and system of clearly defined, irrevocable property rights. Purchasing land in virtual worlds today feels a lot like purchasing land in Manhattan in 1750\. It's also protected from the real-world real estate industry's COVID-induced uncertainty.

Janine Yorio is the head of real estate at Republic, an online investment platform for individual investors who recently launched Republic Realm, a digital real estate fund portfolio. She previously served as a portfolio manager for NorthStar Capital and as the head of real estate production at Standard Hotels.

In virtual worlds, players inhabit an avatar's body and communicate with the world, or "metaverse." They can communicate with other users, earn cryptocurrency by playing games and gambling, purchase art in galleries, attend events and concerts, and various other activities. Cryptovoxels, Axie Infinity, Somnium Space, and The Sandbox (which have not yet launched) are among the other crypto-based virtual worlds. Still, Decentraland is the fastest-growing and most developed of them all.

 

## METAVERSE

With the arrival of the metaverse, the world as we know it today will be drastically altered. The metaverse will give an alternate digital reality in which humans will be able to not only live, work, and play.

 

So, what's the big deal about the metaverse?

Why should you invest in it as an investor? According to Bloomberg, the market size of the metaverse is estimated to reach $800 billion by 2024. This shows how profitable the metaverse could be in the coming years.

 

 

What metaverse investments can you make? What is the best way to get started investing in the metaverse? What are the best tokens to invest in?

 

 

Decentraland (MANA)

MANA, Decentraland's token, is another metaverse token you should about.

Decentraland is a virtual reality platform built on the blockchain that allows users to buy, trade, and build land while also playing games, generating content, and connecting with one another.

Decentraland is the largest virtual universe in the NFT realm, and it is represented by the LAND token, which is a non- fungible ERC-71 token. Each piece of LAND on

Decentraland is unique, and its owners have complete control over what they do with it.

MANA can be used to purchase LAND on the platform. MANA is the major token in Decentraland, and it is used to buy LAND as well. It is used to pay for products and services as a native utility token. It's likewise based on Ethereum and may be purchased and traded in exchange for other cryptocurrencies or fiat currency.

MANA’s total supply is fixed at 2.6 billion MANA. That means that at any given time, there will never be more than

billion MANA.

 

 

The Sandbox (SAND)

SAND is another token to keep an eye on in the metaverse, with a maximum supply of 3 billion. The Sandbox is a virtual world built on the Ethereum blockchain, just like the rest of the platforms in this book.

Players may create, market, and own their game experience on the platform. The SAND token is an ERC-20 utility token that is used for governance, staking, and transactions on the network.

 

 

SuperWorld

SuperWorld is an augmented reality virtual environment that allows users to buy and trade virtual real estate using NFTs – the internet's buzzword.

The company announced that props will be used as the company's loyalty token. Users of SuperWorld will be able to gain access to benefits within the SuperWorld virtual world as a result of the integration. Because the NFTs can uniquely identify digital pieces of land and their owners, buyers hope to profit from virtual real estate investments.

It's similar to Upland and other real-world virtual real estate games.CEO Hrish Lotlikar and Max Woon established SuperWorld in 2017. With the surge in popularity of NFTs, the platform is increasingly being adopted by the general public.

SuperWorld virtual real estate allows you can stake a claim on approximately 64.8 billion virtual parcels of land that are geographically mapped onto the Earth. In SuperWorld, each piece of land is a 100-by-100-meter polygon that correlates to real-world space.

The platform follows the ERC-721 standard for tradable assets, which means that each plot is classified as a collectible and entirely unique asset to buy, sell, trade, or hold due to its digital scarcity. When a user purchases property in SuperWorld, they receive a unique portion of the Ethereum blockchain, as well as revenue opportunities from NFTs, gaming, e-commerce, and advertising activities on their properties.

In a statement, Lotlikar stated that all SuperWorld users should partake in the value created on the platform by any of their activities. He claims that by using Props as a loyalty token, the company is offering active community members with perks as well as a financial stake in the network they are trying to build.

In a statement, Adi Sideman, cofounder of the Props Public Benefit Corp., said he believes in SuperWorld's metaverse vision and that Props integration into an NFT environment and marketplace is an amazing next step for the Props ecosystem.

Artists of different ages and worldwide are taking over the crypto art world, from 18-year-old Fewocious to 87-year-old José Delbo. More than 509,000 NFT artworks were sold In March 2021, totaling more than $85 million in sales. It's a big deal.
